## Document 004: 10月PMI数据解读：景气重回线下，政策效果待释放

Source PDF: all report pdf/10月PMI数据解读：景气重回线下，政策效果待释放.pdf
Report source: 国信证券
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

事项：
10 月份，制造业 PMI为 49.3%，环比下行 0.8个百分点；非制造业 PMI 为 49.7%，环比下行 0.5 个百分点。
评论：
图1：中国 PMI主要数据一览
资料来源：Wind，中采咨询，国信证券经济研究所整理
结论：10月制造业 PMI 下行 0.8 个百分点至 49.3，重回荣枯线下，环比降幅大于近三年同期。生产与新
订单同步回落，生产是主要拖累，但需求不足格局未变。购进和出厂价格仍在扩张区间，但涨幅明显收窄，
10 月 PPI 环比涨幅或有所收窄，同比或继续上行。分行业看，制造业景气分化，中游制造业表现较好，
部分消费类行业已在企稳。非制造业 PMI 下行 0.5 个百分点至 49.7，其中服务业恰好位于荣枯线，建筑
业重回收缩区间。
整体来看，10 月“银十”成色不足，制造业与非制造业均回落至荣枯线下，显示 9 月季末回暖的持续性
不足。向前看，新型政策性金融工具已投放完毕，随着项目落地和配套融资跟进，有望逐步形成实物工作
量；房贷贴息和专项债使用提速等政策效果仍待释放，预计 11月制造业 PMI在荣枯线附近运行。

2026年11月01日

### Page 2

 制造业
10月制造业景气度重回荣枯线下。10月制造业 PMI下行 0.8个百分点至 49.3，同比去年高 0.3 个百分点，
比近三年同期均值低 0.2个百分点。从环比变化看，本月降幅大于近三年同期（环比均值约-0.4 个百分点）。
分贡献度看，生产和新订单是主要拖累。生产、新订单、配送、雇员和原料库存的贡献分别为-0.475、-0.33、
-0.015、0 和 0.02个百分点。
生产与需求同步回落，产需差有所收窄。生产下行 1.9个百分点至 49.8，时隔两个月重回荣枯线下；新订
单下行 1.1个百分点至 49.4，“产需差”收窄 0.8 个百分点至 0.4个百分点，但供强需弱格局延续。“三
大需求”集体下滑：新出口订单下行 1.3 个百分点至 48.7，在手订单下行 1.9 个百分点至 44.1，均处于
收缩区间。
库存分化，价格涨幅收窄。库存方面，原材料库存上行 0.2个百分点至 48.4，连续两个月回升；产成品库
存下行 0.1 个百分点至 47.5，连续三个月下行。与此同时，购进价和出厂价均高位回落。购进价格下行

4.0个百分点至 56.8，出厂价格下行 2.3 个百分点至 51.7，二者仍处扩张区间。价格端上行斜率放缓，10
月 PPI环比涨幅或收窄。
企业预期小幅回落，中小企业景气度下降更多。制造业生产经营活动预期下行 0.4个百分点至 53.4，仍在
荣枯线上。分规模看，大型企业景气度下行 0.4个百分点至 50.2，连续三个月位于扩张区间；中型企业景
气度下行 1.3 个百分点至 48.4，小型企业景气度下行 1.4 个百分点至 47.5，“大小企业差”走阔 1.0 个
百分点至 2.7个百分点。分行业看，17个制造业行业中有 9 个行业 PMI位于荣枯线上，专用设备、化纤橡
塑、石油加工等行业表现较好，黑色金属和纺织业景气度偏低。
 非制造业
10 月非制造业 PMI 下行 0.5 个百分点至 49.7，重回荣枯线下，弱于近年同期（比近三年同期均值低 0.6
个百分点），环比表现也略弱于近三年同期（均值约-0.3个百分点）。结构上，非制造业新订单下行 2.1
个百分点至 44.4，仍处低位；就业人员上行 0.1 个百分点至 46.0，就业景气度仍然偏弱；预期下行 0.8

个百分点至 54.7，仍在荣枯线上。
非制造业价格指标回落。当月价格指标中，购进价格下行 1.0个百分点至 51.5，出厂价格下行 0.8 个百分
点至 49.5、重回荣枯线下，产出与投入的差值上行 0.2 个百分点至-2.0 个百分点，非制造业企业利润率
压力仍然偏大。
行业上，服务业位于线上、建筑业重回线下。服务业 PMI下行 0.2个百分点至 50.0，恰好位于荣枯线。假
期消费相关行业形成支撑，铁路运输、航空运输、邮政、景区服务和文体娱乐等商务活动指数均较上月上
升且位于53%以上；交运中类上行 1.9个百分点至55以上，信息与商务服务中类上行0.4个百分点至 50-55
之间，居民服务中类上行 3.1个百分点但仍处 45-50 之间。
建筑业 PMI下行 2.3个百分点至 48.0，重回收缩区间，显示施工景气度仍偏弱。国庆假期减少施工工作日，
对建筑业形成一定扰动，但新订单下行 5.7 个百分点至 40.0，表明项目需求不足仍是更深层的约束，实物
工作量尚未明显形成。房建中类下行 0.4 个百分点，仍处 45-50 之间，新订单下行 3.7 个百分点至 45 以

下。

制造业PMI 非制造业PMI
10月份制造业PMI季节性回落至收缩区间（49.3）。分项来看，价格分项高位回落但仍维持扩
张，购进和出厂价格分别为  56.8（-4.0）和51.7（-2.3）  ，二者差缩至  5.1pcts  ，中下游利润挤
压边际缓解。生产指数  49.8（-1.9）  、新订单  49.4（-1.1）  ，产需缺口由上月1.2pcts收窄
至  0.4pcts  ，两者均落入收缩区间，指向企业根据订单变化主动下调产出节奏。新出口订单回落

至  48.7（-1.3）  ，不过仍高于过去五年同期水平，显示外需支撑边际减弱但仍具有相对韧性。原
材料库存  48.4（+0.2）  、产成品库存  47.5（-0.1）  ，叠加采购量  48.9  （-2.1）与进口  47.0（-
2.2）  回落，反映订单走弱背景下企业“以销定产”。 高技术  51.3  与装备  50.6  维持扩张，对冲总
量回落，而消费品  48.8  、高耗能  47.3  拖累更加明显，“K型分化”扩大。大型企业  50.2  相对稳
定，中小企业收缩进一步深化，分化加剧。非制造业商务活动指数  49.7（-0.5）  ，服务业  50.0
（-0.2）  ，建筑业  48.0（-2.3）未能延续9月的回升态势，“银十”未形成有效托底，建筑开工
进度偏慢。总体来看，10月回落幅度与历史季节性大致相当，短期扰动（长假与前期备货透支）
叠加需求接续不足是主因，四季度修复仍依赖财政资金加快转化为实物工作量与内需改善落地。

## Document 033: 20261027_投資週報_AI資金壓力擾動市場，科技基本面仍穩健

Source PDF: all report pdf/20261027_投資週報_AI資金壓力擾動市場，科技基本面仍穩健.pdf
Report source: Yuanta (投資週報)
Date: 2026-10-27
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 10 月 27 日
上週重點訊息
◼ AI 投資回報疑慮升溫，資金轉向債市避險：市場對大型科技企業 AI 基礎建設投資規模、融資
需求及未來獲利回收能力的疑慮升溫，導致科技股賣壓擴大，10 月 22 日 Nasdaq 指數下跌
約 2.6%。 隨著市場風險偏好下降 ， 避險資金轉向美國公債 ，10 年期公債殖利率單日下降約 19
個基點，創 2023 年 3 月以來最大單日跌幅，並於 23 日回升至 4.99%。市場關注焦點逐漸由
AI 投資規模轉向資本支出效益，後續大型科技企業財報及現金流表現，將成為評估 AI 產業評

價合理性的重要依據。
◼ 中東衝突推升通膨風險：中東衝突持續升溫，伊朗支持的葉門胡塞武裝攻擊沙烏地阿拉伯，引
發市場對原油供應中斷的擔憂，推升布蘭特原油價格於10 月 23 日升至每桶 116.72 美元，單
週上漲約 6.2%，創 2022 年以來最高週收盤水準。能源價格攀升加劇全球通膨壓力，並壓抑
市場風險偏好。
◼ 法國政治動盪加劇財政疑慮，歐洲主權債風險重新受到關注：法國因生活成本上升爆發大規模
抗議，進一步加深市場對政府財政整頓能力的疑慮。10 月 19 日法國 10 年期公債殖利率上升
約 10 個基點至 4.88%附近，與德國公債利差擴大至約 144 個基點，創 2012 年以來最高收盤
水準 ， 法股單日下跌約1.5%， 歐元同步走弱 。 市場擔憂政治不確定性可能削弱財政改革進度 ，
並透過主權債評價影響銀行業資產負債表。後續須關注法國財政政策、歐洲主權債利差及銀行
融資成本變化。
市場展望
◼ 科技股短線宜審慎，中期聚焦基本面穩健的 AI 企業：近期 AI 資本支出、融資需求及評價調整

使科技股波動加劇 ， 但先進製程與半導體設備需求仍具韌性 ，AI 中長期成長趨勢未改變 。 隨市
場焦點由成長題材轉向投資效益，企業營收與訂單能見度、自由現金流及資本效率的重要性提
升 ， 具技術優勢 、 財務穩健及獲利能見度的企業相對有利 ， 而高度依賴外部融資者宜審慎看待 。
◼ 美債避險功能重現，中天期債券風險報酬相對均衡：市場風險偏好降溫時，美國公債仍具一定
避險效果。儘管 Fed 貨幣政策路徑與能源價格增添利率不確定性，短天期債券易受政策利率
牽動，超長天期債券面臨財政赤字、國債供給及期限溢價等壓力，相較之下，中天期美債的風
險報酬較為均衡。信用債方面，投資等級企業基本面及現金流仍具韌性，後續仍可持續關注。

## Document 101: 261027_daiwa_unimicron

### Page 1

Summary: Our first take on Unimicron’s 3Q26 results (released on 27 October) is that 
earnings were better than expected, driven by stronger-than-expected non-operating 
gains. We attribute the upside primarily to a smaller-than-feared valuation loss on UMC in 
3Q26. In addition, the company mentioned that other investments, including Unimicron 
shares held through investment vehicles, contributed c.TWD3bn of mark-to-market gains in 
3Q26. As for the core business, 3Q26 revenue reached TWD52.7bn (up 23% QoQ, up 
55% YoY), with gross margin expanding 4.0ppt QoQ to 28.8% and operating margin rising 
4.5ppt QoQ to 20.0%, supported by improving profitability in ABF substrates and HDI. We 
believe the profitability performance was broadly in line with the latest buy-side 
expectations. Looking ahead, while the company does not provide numerical guidance, 
revenue and gross margin should continue to improve in 4Q26. Given Unimicron’s leading 
position across most CSP AI ASIC projects, Nvidia GPUs, and EMIB-T, we believe the 
company should continue to benefit from a favourable pricing environment amid the 
current supply-demand dynamics and ongoing specification upgrades, including larger 
substrate body sizes and technology migration. We have a Buy (1) rating on Unimicron 
with a 12M TP of TWD1,500, based on a target PER of 35x applied to our 1-year-forward 
EPS forecast. Key downside risk: weaker-than-expected demand.

What’s the impact: 
Below is our first take on Unimicron’s 3Q26 results (released on 27 October). We will 
review our model in due course.

• 3Q26 results beat expectations. Unimicron’s 3Q26 earnings came in at TWD10.1bn 
(EPS: TWD6.15), beating our estimate of TWD4.2bn and Bloomberg consensus of 
TWD6.7bn by 140% and 49%, respectively, mainly due to higher-than-expected non-
operating gains. In our previous note, Unimicron recognised c.TWD8bn of mark-to-
market gains on UMC (2303 TT, TWD122.5, Neutral [3], covered by Rick Hsu) in 2Q26. 
We had originally expected Unimicron to recognise c.TWD5bn of valuation losses in 
3Q26, given UMC’s share-price performance in July. However, the actual valuation loss 
of c.TWD0.7bn was substantially better than feared. In addition, the company attributed 
part of the strength to c.TWD3bn of other mark-to-market gains, including gains related 
to Unimicron shares held through its investment vehicles. This is broadly consistent with 
the reported 3Q26 total non-operating contribution of c.TWD2.6bn after accounting for 
the UMC valuation loss and other non-operating items.

Unimicron Technology Corp (3037 TT)

• Looking at the core business, 3Q26 revenue reached TWD52.7bn (up 23% QoQ, up 
55% YoY), with gross margin expanding 4.0ppt QoQ to 28.8% and operating margin 
rising 4.5ppt QoQ to 20.0%, supported by improving profitability in ABF substrates and 
HDI. With stronger operating leverage, operating profit surged 58% QoQ to 
TWD10.53bn. We believe the gross-margin and operating-margin performance was 
broadly in line with recent buy-side expectations for modestly improving profitability in 
ABF substrates and HDI, given that ABF margin was already high at c.40% in 2Q26, 
alongside a higher revenue contribution from a large end customer. We estimate ABF 
substrate margin at c.42-44% in 3Q26 (vs. c.40% in 2Q26).

• Business outlook. While the company does not provide numerical guidance, revenue 
and gross margin should continue to improve in 4Q26 amid the current substrate supply 
shortage. In addition, the company is aggressively expanding capacity at its Guangfu 
and Yangmei plants over 2027-29, with roughly c.30% annual ABF capacity expansion, 
which should become a key growth driver for Unimicron. Given Unimicron’s leading 
position across most CSP AI ASIC projects, Nvidia GPUs, and EMIB-T, we believe the 
company should continue to benefit from a favourable pricing environment amid the 
current supply-demand dynamics and ongoing specification upgrades, including larger 
substrate body sizes and technology migration.

What we recommend: 
We have a Buy (1) rating on Unimicron with a 12M TP of TWD1,500, based on a target 
PER of 35x applied to our 1-year-forward EPS forecast. Key downside risk: less-than-
expected demand.

### Page 1

Powertech Technology

Up-cycle level of earnings to continue; 
reiterate Buy

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 500.00 TWD | Price: 254.50 TWD

Reiterate Buy on above-trend growth and margins 
We believe Powertech Technology Inc. (PTI) can continue to benefit from the AI boom 
and an upturn in the memory chip industry. 3Q results clearly confirm record-high 
revenue (NT$25bn; up 27% YoY) and an above-trend gross margin (24%; normal high 
teen% or 20% range). 4Q guidance (for packaging/testing businesses) also remained 
bullish: DRAM – tight supply vs strong demand; NAND – order increase for more value-
added NAND and enterprise SSD; and logic – mix improvement with FC-BGA and price 
hike (following cost increase). Key customers are Micron, Kioxia, Solidigm (owned by SK 
Hynix) and various Greater China logic chip makers that require outsourced packaging 
and testing services. As we have already assumed up-cycle earnings level for 2027-28, 
our EPS estimates remain largely unchanged. We maintain our PO of NT$500 (27x 2027-
28E P/E applied). Reiterate Buy.  
Key takeaways from 3Q results  
Five takeaways from 3Q results: (1) strong execution in newly growing packaging orders 
for high-end memory (low-power / high-speed DRAM, enterprise SSD, 200+ layers 
NAND), (2) increasing exposure to more advanced logic (through FC-BGA, etc), (3) a well-
balanced sales mix (DRAM: 20% of total sales; NAND: 28%; SiP/modules 9%; logic 43%), 
(4) notably improved gross margins (limited increase in manufacturing costs vs strong 
top-line growth seen), and (5) solid FCF generation (EBITDA: c.NT$8bn vs capex: 
NT$5bn; expected annual dividend yield: 3-4% range).

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
Headquartered in Taiwan, PTI is one of the largest OSAT 
firms, which packages and tests memory and logic chips. It 
has around 18,000 employees. The logic chip-related 
business operates under Greatek Electronics (fully 
consolidated into earnings). Management's new focus areas 
in packaging/testing are PLP, TSV, 3D NAND, low-power 
DRAM, SSD, etc.

Investment Rationale 
Our Buy rating is based on strong earnings growth, driven 
by more value-added memory and even logic packaging 
solutions. 1Q-3Q26 earnings have already hit upcycle levels, 
posting a strong YoY growth (largely driven by NAND/SSD). 
We believe the memory super-cycle and customers' 
ongoing outsourcing trend (Micron, Solidigm/Hynix, Kioxia 
and Greater China's logic chipmakers) using Powertech's 
proven backend solutions should be the key contributors to 
strong earnings growth by 2028.

### Page 13

Price objective basis & risk 
Powertech Technology (PWOGF) 
Our PO of NT$500 is derived from 27x P/E based on 2027-28E EPS. This is 10-20% 
higher than 2024-25 peak levels (low- to mid-20x). Since we expect 50%+ higher EPS in 
2026-28E vs 2024-25 on the back of record-high sales/margin, we believe fair multiples 
should be at least on par with the previous peak level. True, the long-term historical 
average P/E came in the range of 10-20x, but the memory super-cycle and AI boom 
should warrant above-trend earnings growth and consequently over 20x fair P/E. 
Taiwan's OSAT stocks also trade at 20-30x P/E.

Upside risk is mostly related to strong packaging orders from memory chipmakers and 
even logic chip suppliers.

Downside risk is mostly associated with an unexpected memory industry downturn or AI 
bubble (e.g., significant packaging order cut).

Chroma Ate Inc.  | Asia Pacific
Momentum Seen Sustained into
2027
SL T, photonics (transceiver+CPO), and power testing should
continue to drive growth into 2027, with contributions from FT
handlers and burn-in ovens likely emerging as well. Stay OW.
3Q26 miss on GM: See details in Results Summary
Growth momentum seen sustained into 4Q26 and 2027: Management indicated
that the strong business momentum observed in 3Q26 is likely to stay intact going

forward, driven by both power testing and semi & photonics businesses. Normalized
GM remains at ~60% (2026 guidance of ~60% GM implies GM back to ~60% in
4Q26 vs. 57.5% in 3Q26).
• Power testing: 60-70% of revenue is AI-related for 2026, and it is expected
to be sustained into 2027. HVDC testing tools could be the major driver, and
it has already received orders from customers.
• SLT: 9M26 revenue doubled on a YoY basis. SL T demand used to focus on

one major customer, but management indicated that it has added AVGO and
MRVL as new customers. Moreover, demand in 4Q26/1H27 should be mostly
related to inference applications, including CPU/ASIC/LPU.
• Photonics: 9M26 revenue grew by 4x on  a YoY basis. Demand from
transceivers should remain solid and CPO should grow notably in 2027.
Chroma focuses on Insertion 3/4o and shipments for the mass production
line next year  are seen reaching 4-5x vs. that for the pilot line this year.

Moreover, it has added another customer for a pilot run in 9M27 and mass
production in 4Q27. At the industry level, if insertion is excluded from mass
production, Insertion 3 tool demand should be higher. Opportunities will
likely be much larger when CPO penetrates the scale-up network.
• New businesses: FT handlers have been qualified by multiple HPC/CSP
customers and orders have been placed. Burn-in ovens have secured the first
CSP customer, with more HPC customers expected to follow next year.

These should be new addressable markets for Chroma in the coming years.
Stay OW: We believe Chroma should continue to benefit from the AI compute/
networking/power testing opportunities due to its strong customer relationship,
solid execution and outstanding technology expertise. Our PT of NT$2,800 is based
on 40x 2027e P/E, implying a PEG of 0.6 (vs. 1.0 over the past decade).

3Q26 miss on GM:
Off revenue of  NT$17,926mn (+32% QoQ/+180% YoY), GM contracted 3ppt QoQ, to
57.5%, below our estimate at 60.5% and consensus at 62.7%, mainly owing to a less
favorable product mix.
Opex of NT$3,141mn (opex ratio 17.5%) was lower than our estimate at NT$3,797mn (opex
ratio 21.2%) and consensus at NT$3,321mn (opex ratio 21.7%), reflecting operating
leverage.
This led to operating profit of NT$7,167mn (OPM up 1.2ppt QoQ, to 40.0%), 2% higher

than our estimate at NT$7,040mn (OPM 39.3%) and 14% higher than consensus at NT
$6,281mn (OPM 41.0%).

$13.37), 2% short of our estimate at NT$5,790mn (EPS NT$13.71) and 6% short of
consensus at NT$5,317mn (EPS NT$12.59).

A Closer Look
Test instrument and ATS segment looks set to reach another
record high in 2026
Revenue for the segment increased 55% YoY to NT$10,546mn (37% of the mix) in 2025.
We estimate it will rise 118% YoY to NT$24,564mn (40% of the mix) in 2026. The ever-
increasing power consumption of AI clusters should continue to drive demand for higher
spec testing tools. Along with our forecast of a 75% CAGR for global power supply unit

demand in the coming three years (see our report), this should remain a major driver for
Chroma, in our view.

Multiple drivers in the semiconductor business
System-level testing  (SL T) business
SL T should benefit from the capex cycle of NVIDIA's Rubin generation GPU, AMD's MI450
GPU and more ASIC accelerators.  Management pointed out that discrete testing tools are
still preferred  by major customers.  We are also seeing more HPC chip designers adopting

the system level testing process to make sure defects won't be incorporated into the AI
systems. We believe Chroma, as the first mover in this market, should benefit from
increasing chip volume, as well as higher TDP.
Metrology business
The metrology business should continue to benefit from current advanced packaging
architectures from its foundry and OSAT customers. It is confident that the performance
of its tools can win future architecture projects, which our industry checks suggest should

have higher metrology density.

### Page 4

M
Idea
4
Exhibit 4: Chroma –  Offerings for Metrology in Advanced Packaging
Source: Chroma
Chroma's metrology equipment employs white light interferometry technology. The basic
working principle is that white light is emitted on the reference mirror and the sample,
which are placed at the same distance from the light source. The reflected beams from
the reference mirror and sample surface are then received by a light sensor.
Interference occurs when two light beams collide, which could cause  them to either

strengthen or weaken, depending on the phase of the two light beams. Thus, by
measuring the intensity of the combined light beams, the distance between light sensor
and multiple points on the target surface can be calculated through an algorithm. This can
then be used to measure the dimensions of the structure or the alignment of layers.
Exhibit 5: Concept of Light Interference
Source: Keyence
Exhibit 6: Setup of Interferometer
Source: Wikipedia
CPO contribution to start from 2026

Chroma has been qualified for optical engine testing in Insertion 3 and system-level
testing in Insertion 4, and there might be incremental opportunities if supply chain players'
progress falls behind for testing equipment in Insertion 2. Over the longer term, Chroma
also  aspires to participate in the PIC optical/electrical/reliability testing in Insertion 1.

FT handlers to add another revenue stream

believe this is a natural extension of its expertise and customer relationships accumulated
in SL Ts. We expect Chroma to gain more traction in FT handlers for AI HPCs in 2027.

Valuation Methodology and Risks
Chroma Ate Inc. (2360.TW)
Base case, 40x 2027e P/E. We use P/E as our valuation methodology because we think it better
captures Chroma's earnings growth momentum. The stock traded at an average valuation of
~19x P/E during 2014-25 with an operating profit CAGR of 20%, implying a PEG of 1.0. We think
our target multiple of 40x is justified by the 68% operating profit CAGR we project for

2025-28.
Risks to Upside
n Better-than-expected macro conditions
n Stronger semi and photonics and power testing demand
n Margin improvement via better product mix
n Better opex control
Risks to Downside
n Macro slowdown
n Softer-than-expected AI capex
n Slower progress in the advanced packaging capacity expansion
n Weaker consumer electronics and automobile related demand
n Margin contraction arising from lack of revenue scale and increasing opex

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

產品組合

股價相對大盤走勢

長榮航太 (2645 TT)  Trading Buy

預期波音 737 系列交機速度將略為放緩。  
投資建議 ：雖然 2026 年長榮航太的維修業務持續擴張，但考量
波音將維持 737 系列月均交機一段時間，可能會影響長榮航太
的製造部門復甦情形，故給予長榮航太 Trading Buy 的投資建
議。  
波音 737 系列月交機將維持一段時間，調整長榮航太後續製造
的營運預估： 波音於 10/27/2026 公告 3Q26 財報，並於法說會
上提及 :「當前需維持 737 系列月均交機量穩定在 47 架一段時
間，後續管理階層預期交機提升可能要到 2H27 才會上升至 52
架」，使得長榮航太的製造部門復甦仍需等待一段時間，故研
究部一併調整長榮航太後續的營運預估。  
3Q26 先前的維修漲價陸續貢獻，營運維持高檔： 3Q26 長榮航
太自 2H24 起陸續針對維修業務進行漲價後，加上全球機隊持
續供不應求，增加維修機率與維修產值，惟製造部門雖然波音
已將 737 系列交機上升至 47 架 /月，但短期拉貨力道仍有限，
使得長榮航太的製造業務與轉投資公司營運維持低檔，營運仍
以維修業務為主， 3Q26 營收 52.75 億元， QoQ+1.02% ，預估稅
後純益 8.31 億元， QoQ+10.34% ， EPS 2.22 元。  
2027 年空中巴士首件交付、無人機新訂單，有望成為中長期營
運新動能： 2027 年長榮航太除了持續受惠維修業務與波音交機
的逐年擴張之外，在新訂單的部分，先前與空中巴士的新訂單，
預估 1Q27 將迎來首件交付，並待後續取得特殊製程認證後，
2H27 有望迎來新訂單的量產，以及無人機訂單也有望成為長榮
航太新一波的營運動能，預估 2027 年營收 224.86 億元，
YoY+7.85% ，稅後純益 37.61 億元， YoY+17.06% ，EPS 10.04 元。

3 群益投顧 
圖一、全球商用飛機平均機齡

資料來源 : IATA ，長榮航太  
波音 737 系列月交機將維持一段時間，調整長榮航太後續製造的營運預估：
波音於 10/27/2026 公告 3Q26 財報，並於法說會上提及 :「當前需維持 737 系
列月均交機量穩定在 47 架一段時間，後續管理階層預期交機提升可能要到
2H27 才會上升至 52 架」，使得長榮航太的製造部門復甦仍需等待一段時
間，故研究部一併調整長榮航太後續的營運預估。  
2027 年空中巴士首件交付、無人機新訂單，有望成為中長期營運新動能：  
2027 年長榮航太除了持續受惠維修業務與波音交機的逐年擴張之外，在新
訂單的部分，先前與空中巴士的新訂單，預估 1Q27 將迎來首件交付，並待
後續取得特殊製程認證後， 2H27 有望迎來新訂單的量產，以及無人機訂單
也有望成為長榮航太新一波的營運動能，預估 2027 年營收 224.86 億元，
YoY+7.85% ，稅後純益 37.61 億元， YoY+17.06% ， EPS 10.04 元。  
投資建議：  
雖然 2026 年長榮航太的維修業務持續擴張，但考量波音將維持 737 系列月
均交機一段時間，可能會影響長榮航太的製造部門復甦情形，故給予長榮航
太 Trading Buy 的投資建議。

7 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】  
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間 ≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy) 5% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等  
預期近期股價將處於盤整  
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】  
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。   
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。  
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」 、 「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。  
停止推薦情境：  
1.  達目標價。  
2.  雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。  
3.  推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】  
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」 。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

### Page 1

두산에너빌리티 (034020)

EARNINGS REVIEW Company Analysis | 건설/원전 | 2026. 10. 30  
숫자로 증명하는 중

3Q26 Review: 에너빌부문 믹스개선, 두산밥캣 호실적 
두산에너빌리티 3분기 매출액은 4조 4,494억원으로 전년동기대비 14.7% 증가, 컨센
서스에 부합했다. 영업이익은 2,612억원으로 전년동기대비 90.6% 증가, 컨센서스 영
업이익 2,464억원 대비 6.0% 상회하는 호실적을 기록했다. 에너빌리티부문의 원자
력, 가스터빈 매출 증가에 따른 믹스 개선 효과와 두산밥캣의 가격정책 효과에 힘입은 
마진 개선으로 안정적인 실적 기조가 이어지고 있어 긍정적이다. 분기 환평가손실로 
인해 순이익은 여타 수출기업과 유사하게 컨센서스 대비 하회했다. 에너빌부문의 수주
는 누계 10조원으로 목표대비 75%를 채웠으며, 수주 대기중인 안건들을 고려할 때 
당분간 수주 업사이클이 몇년간 지속될 것임이 분명한 시점이다. 
기회가 아닌 경쟁력, 모멘텀이 아닌 수주  
두산에너빌리티에 대해 투자의견 Buy 유지하나, 목표주가를 95,000원으로 하향했다. 
목표 EBITDA는 2035년 EBITDA에 WACC 8%를 적용, 목표 EV/EBITDA는 Global 
Peers인 GE Vernova의 2026년 EV/EBITDA 멀티플에서 자회사 실적 변동성을 고
려해 10% 할인 적용했다. 호실적과 수주 증가에도 GEV의 멀티플 감소와 순차입금 
증가로 인한 NAV 감소를 밸류에이션에 반영한 결과이다. 전일 주가는 한수원의 체코 
원전 시공사 협상 결렬로 인해 7.65% 하락 마감했다. 시공 수주는 아쉽지만 두산에너
빌리티의 체코 원전 기자재 납품에는 전혀 영향이 없으며, 글로벌 전력 수요 증가로 경
제적해자인 대형 가스 터빈 수급 쇼티지는 당분간 지속될 전망임을 고려할 때 전일 주
가는 낙폭 과대로 판단한다. 1) 두산에너빌리티의 가스터빈은 이미 2031년 출하 물량
에 대한 예약 주문이 마무리되고 있으며, 2) 또한 AP1000의 우량한 수주 파이프라인
과, 3) 뉴스케일파워, 테라파워, 롤스로이스, X Energy 등의 SMR 제작을 위한 협업 역
시 지속되고 있다는 점에 주목한다. 전일 기사를 통해 뉴스케일파워 SMR 1호 제작 계
약을 체결했다는 소식을 전했으며, 이제 에너지 트렌지션은 기회가 아닌 회사의 경쟁
력으로, 가스터빈과 원전은 모멘텀이 아니라 수주로 입증되고 있다.  
 Analyst 김세련 
sally.kim@ls-sec.co.kr

## Document 309: Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds

Source PDF: all report pdf/Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Compelling risk-reward in 
US 10-year bonds  
Summary 
Event: The US 10 -year government bond yield has surged 
past 5.0% to a 19-year high and has now entered our three -
month forecast range of 5.0-5.25%. At the time of writing, the 
10-year yield is trading at 5.2%, its highest since June 2007’s 
high of 5.32%. The next resistance levels are the 2002 high 
of 5.47% and the 2001 high of 5.53%.  
We view the risk/reward for the US 10-year government 
bond after the recent yield spike as  compelling and we 
are initiating a long opportunistic idea at the current 
yield. As the table on the right shows, if the 10 -year yield 
rises to 6%, investors would face less than 1% in negative 
total returns over a one-year period, whereas if the yield falls 
below 5.0%, they would gain at least 6% in total returns.  
While we continue to prefer the 3-7-year bond maturity range 
for income generation (the US 5-year bond yield is also 
above 5%), and broadly prefer corporate over government 
bonds amid solid corporate fundamentals, the US 10 -year 
government bond yield has finally reached reasonable levels 
for investors looking for slightly longer maturities. 
Rationale: We expect the US 10-year government bond yield 
to ease to 4.75-5.0% over a 12-month period due to: 
Easing oil prices and inflation over next 12 months: While 
the yield is likely to remain elevated in the next three months 
due to elevated oil prices caused by geopolitical uncertainty, 
heavy AI investment-driven bond issuance and a resilient US 
economy, it is likely to ease towards the 4.75 -5.0% range 
over 12  months as oil prices moderate either due to a 
resolution in the Middle East or a rerouting of Middle East 
energy supplies and as the impact of tariffs fade.  
We expect s hallower Fed rate hikes than market s: We 
expect a total of 50bps of Fed rate hikes by June 2027, before 
a cut in H2 as US inflation eventually eases towards the Fed’s 
2% target. This  rate trajectory is shallower than c. 95bps of 
further hikes priced by markets over the next 12 months. 
 The benchmark US 10-year government bond yield has 
risen to its highest since 2007, offering medium-term 
investors a compelling risk-reward trade-off 
US 10-year government bond yield and previous highs

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered

At current levels, the US 10-year government bond yield 
offers investors compelling risk-reward trade off

year Developed Market government bonds from shifts in 
their yields; grey dotted lines roughly denote current spot 
yield levels

### Page 2

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Investor positioning adjustment: In the very near-term, the 
US 10 -year bond yield has room to move lower as hedge 
funds using systematic strategies (CTAs) are extremely short 
US government bonds, raising the chance of short -covering 
if upcoming jobs and inflation data miss estimates. 
A shallow Fed hiking cycle  
We expect the shallowest Fed hiking cycle in almost 
three decades. This should enable the US 10-year yield to 
ease towards 4.75 -5.0% over a 12 -month period. Following 
this month’s increase, we forecast two further 25bp hikes by 
June 2027, followed by a cut in H2 2027. Markets price at 
least three more hikes by June and a strong probability of a 
fourth by September. Our projected 75bp s of total hikes this 
cycle compares with 525bps of hikes in 2022–23, 225bps in 
2015–18, 425bps in 2004–06 and 175bp in 1999–2000. The 
last smaller mid-cycle move was a single 25bps hike in 1997. 
Why we expect a more cautious Fed 
The latest hike restored the Fed’s credibility after renewed 
oil-price pressures, elevated inflation and a balanced labour 
market prompted a unanimous policy vote. However, the 
following factors argue against aggressive tightening: 
Supply-driven inflation: Energy costs and AI-infrastructure 
constraints, rather than excess demand, are driving prices. 
Higher rates cannot repair supply and may weaken growth. 
Labour is no longer the main inflation source:  Labour 
supply and demand are better balanced, wage growth has 
moderated and unemployment is near full employment. AI 
further weakens the traditional Phillips Curve relationship 
between unemployment and inflation. 
Disinflation should resume:  Energy pressures may fade 
after the US mid-term elections, while the one-off inflationary 
effects of tariffs should diminish after 2025–26. 
AI raises the neutral rate , but dampens inflation:  
Investment and productivity may lift the neutral rate ( r*), the 
rate consistent with stable inflation and full employment, 
while efficiency gains limit wage-price spirals. 
Markets are already tightening  financial conditions : 
Higher bond yields have raised borrowing costs and 
restrained activity, doing part of the Fed’s work. 
The cycle began from a higher base:  The Fed funds rate 
started at 3.75%, versus 0.25% in the previous two cycles 
and 1% before that, reducing additional tightening required. 
Monetary transmission has weakened:  Wealthier 
households drive more spending but are less rate -sensitive, 
while lower -income consumers are already constrained. 
Further hikes may , therefore, curb demand less effectively 
while causing disproportionate harm to low-income families. 
 We expect a shallower Fed rate hiking cycle compared 
with market expectations 
Our policy rate outlook for the Fed, ECB and BoJ

expected rates path over the next 12m

tariffs, long-term inflation expectations remain subdued

### Page 3

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Small business conditions are deteriorating:  Higher 
financing and energy costs are weakening sentiment, as 
reflected in the latest Beige Book . The renewed 
underperformance of US small capitalisation (S&P 600) and 
mid capitalisation (S&P 400) equity indices  versus the large 
cap S&P 500 index over the past couple of months also show 
rising pressure on small businesses. 
Fiscal and financial -stability risks are rising:  Aggressive 
Fed tightening would increase US debt-servicing costs as the 
US Treasury seeks cheaper funding through shorter-maturity 
issuance, while potentially amplifying financial-system stress. 
Upside risk to bond yields 
Our view of lower bond yields carries the following risks: 
Persistent inflation:  Repeated supply shocks, strong AI -
related investment demand and tighter immigration controls 
could keep inflation above target and force steeper Fed rate 
hikes. With unemployment near the Fed’s estimate of full 
employment, a sharp drop in labour supply could intensify 
wage pressures and destabilise inflation expectations . We 
note that long-term expectations remain contained for now. 
Geopolitical escalation:  An intensification of geopolitical 
conflicts after the mid -term elections could lift energy prices 
and inflation. However, a Democrat -controlled or divided 
Congress may constrain US foreign-policy initiatives through 
tighter budgets, while Republicans could turn less supportive 
as attention shifts to the 2028 presidential race. 
Higher neutral rate: AI investments could raise the neutral 
rate (r*), turning current monetary policy settings 
accommodative. This could necessitate further rate hikes. 
However, the Fed has barely raised its nominal neutral rate 
estimate, lifting it to 3.2% in September from 3.0% a year ago. 
Bottomline: The outlook for rates remains geopolitics 
dependent, with all Fed meetings live . However, based on 
our core scenario, we see the US 10-year yield settling lower 
in the 4.75-5.0% in 6-12 months. A shallow Fed hiking cycle 
due to expected disinflation offers investors a reasonably 
attractive risk-reward in US 10-year government bonds. 
US 30-year government bonds , on the other hand, may 
continue to see elevated yields as the US fiscal deficit is 
unlikely to have improved next year, and may look much 
worse. As a result, “term premium” may keep the ultra -long 
end elevated. Short of some unorthodox policy to artifici ally 
cap them lower, the 30-year yield is likely to continue to drift 
higher towards the 5.5-5.75% range into H2 2027. 
— Jonathan Liang, CIO for Fixed Income and FX   
Rajat Bhattacharya, Senior Investment Strategist 
Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

## Document 310: Consumer Checkpoint_ Strength in numbers September saw solid spending across many categories and a strong start to the holidays. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Consumer Checkpoint_ Strength in numbers September saw solid spending across many categories and a strong start to the holidays. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13026023     1

Economy 
Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers 
09 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Consumer spending remained very solid in September. Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), or 5.1% excluding gasoline, both the third strongest growth rates over the past four 
years. Month-over-month (MoM) spending was more muted, especially without gas. 
• Spending shows considerable breadth: the vast majority of discretionary retail and services categories are showing growth. 
Moreover, this strength extends across income cohorts, with the ongoing recovery of lower- and middle-income households' 
spending growth. 
• With the winter months fast approaching, holiday spending is already increasing at a solid rate, though it's still early. In Bank of 
America deposit data through September, buy now, pay later (BNPL) usage is rising but not at a rate that is overly concerning. 
However, nearly one in every five households that use BNPL has a repayment of over $500.

Consumer Checkpoint is a regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It aims to provide a holistic and real-time estimate of US 
consumers’ spending and their financial well-being, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America proprietary data. Such data is not 
intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, financial conditions or performance of 
Bank of America. 
A solid September for spending   
Consumer spending continued to look solid in September. Bank of America total aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), while spending excluding gasoline increased 5.1% YoY. Notably, both are the third 
strongest growth rates in the past four years. Seasonally-adjusted total card spending rose 0.2% month-over-month (MoM), 
while spending excluding gasoline was little changed. Overall, spending growth remains well above the 2025 average rate 
(Exhibit 1). 
Exhibit 1: Total card spending per household rose 6.2% YoY and 5.1% YoY excluding gasoline in September 
Total card spending growth per household, based on Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card data (monthly, MoM%, seasonally adjusted (SA)) and 
(monthly, YoY%, non-SA, right-hand side (rhs))

Most spending categories are growing  
One striking thing about recent card spending, in our view, is that it is well balanced across categories. For example, both 
spending on retail (excluding gasoline) and services grew strongly YoY in September, at 3.5% and 5.4%, respectively. Meanwhile, 
retail spending is showing notably better momentum than in 2025 (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: Spending is rising across retail and services 
Retail (excluding gasoline) and services card spending per household (monthly, seasonally adjusted, index January 2025 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Well balanced across “nice-to-have” and “need-to-have” retail categories  
Is the momentum in retail spending reflective of “nice-to-have” discretionary or “need-to-have” necessity spending? In retail, 
this is a nuanced question, as many categories sit in a gray area between “necessity” and “discretionary”. General merchandise 
stores, for example, sell both essentials like groceries as well as nice-to-have electronics or even jewelry.  
In Exhibit 3, we account for this by grouping retail spending into “more discretionary” (furniture, electronics, building materials, 
online retailers, clothing retailers and miscellaneous stores), “partly discretionary” (auto, health and personal care retailers, 
general merchandise) and “mostly necessity” (gas and groceries). Even within these categories the edges will be blurred, but the 
overall impression is that retail spending growth is widely distributed, with spending rising across all fronts. One caveat: “mostly 
discretionary” spending has been making a smaller contribution since June. 
Exhibit 3: Retail spending is distributed across discretionary and necessity categories  
Percentage points contribution to YoY growth by type of retail spending (three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data. See methodology for category aggregation definitions.  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Looking at the share of “more discretionary” retail spending categories that are increasing YoY underscores that growth is well 
balanced and broad-based (Exhibit 4). Non-store and online retailers and miscellaneous stores saw some of the fastest growth, 
while furniture and building materials were among the laggards, though other durable categories showed some strength.

While some of this spending growth likely reflects the impact of higher prices pushing up spending across a swathe of

For services, growth is driven mostly by discretionary spending  
In services spending, on the other hand, the boundaries between discretionary and necessity spending are often clearer. Travel 
spending (e.g., airlines, lodging) can be thought of as, by and large, discretionary. At the same time, utility bills, rent and 
insurance payments are necessities.  
And in services, discretionary spending remains the clear driver, accounting for over three-quarters of YoY spending growth in 
September (Exhibit 6). Leisure and housing services are major components of growth, though travel and restaurants also made 
positive contributions. However, as we noted last month strength in airline spending largely reflects price rises rather than 
transactions growth (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Still sizzling).  
Exhibit 6: Services spending growth has been mostly driven by discretionary spending over the past year

Lower-income households’ spending outpaced all but the top 5% 
The breadth in spending growth across categories is also evident across income cohorts. In September, lower-income 
households’ total card spending growth was 5.5% YoY, while the equivalent figures for middle- and higher-income households 
were 4.9% and 4.8% YoY, respectively. The top 5% of households by income continue to show the fastest spending growth, 
5.9% in September, though this has eased somewhat (Exhibit 7). 
Overall, the divergences across income cohorts are much smaller than they were in 2025 and the first three months of this year. 
Just focusing on spending excluding key necessities (gasoline, food and utilities) doesn’t meaningfully change the picture of 
convergence across income cohorts (Exhibit 8).  
Exhibit 7: The top 5% saw the strongest spending growth of any 
income cohort… 
Total card spending per household by income tercile and the top 5% 
(three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 8: …though discretionary spending for the top 5% has eased 
some since June 
Discretionary card spending per household by income tercile and the top 
5% (three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The strength in lower- and middle-income households’ discretionary spending is occurring despite necessity spending shifting 
from a tailwind to a headwind over the past year amid rising gas prices (Exhibit 9). In our view, a key component of why these 
households have been able to combine rising necessity spending with strength in discretionary is the labor market and a 
recovery in their after-tax wage growth (Exhibit 10).  
In September, lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY from 4.7% YoY in August, while growth for middle- and higher-
income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. These changes were modest, and lower-income households are 
continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” pattern we 
observed over much of 2025 and 2026.  
Exhibit 9: Necessity spending has gone from a tailwind to a 
headwind across all income cohorts

a narrowing gap

Should we be concerned about the easing in household spending and after-tax wage growth for those with the top 5% of 
incomes? For now, probably not, as in our view this spending growth is likely being driven by wealth effects rather than wage 
gains. Absent a sharp correction in equity markets, there is little reason to expect them to retrench their spending. However, 
developments here are worth watching as this cohort comprises a significant proportion of overall consumer spending. They also 
tend to make outsized contributions to several discretionary spending categories (Exhibit 11).  
Exhibit 11: The top 5% of households by income make up a disproportionate amount of spending in highly discretionary categories 
Share of spending by the top 5% of households by income across select discretionary categories (three-month moving average to September 2026, %)

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
With summer gone, thoughts turn to the holidays and BNPL… 
Now that summer is over, the next big gauge of consumer momentum is how people spend during the run-up to, and over, the 
holiday season. It is early days, but September spending on holiday items ran well ahead of 2025 (Exhibit 12). Given the backdrop 
of firmer after-tax wage growth and breadth across retail and services categories, this is perhaps not surprising. However, some 
of this spending strength could be due to rising prices as opposed to more purchases.

  I NSTI TUTE

A concern often raised as we move into the holidays is whether consumers will lean on less traditional forms of credit such as 
buy now, pay later (BNPL) to finance their spending. Frequent BNPL users tend to have higher credit card utilization rates (read 
more in: Do consumers have wiggle room to absorb higher gas prices?).  
Looking at the share of households in Bank of America deposit data with a BNPL payment, we do tend to see a seasonal rise 
over the holidays (Exhibit 13), though in past years, these increases have quickly reversed. Further, while the underlying trend in 
BNPL usage is upwards, there are no signs of a sharp acceleration. We do, however, see some evidence that BNPL is being used 
to finance more expensive purchases, with the share of households making repayments above $500 rising, albeit relatively slowly 
(Exhibit 14).

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

## Document 356: Industrials_ AI Jitters Versus Booming Orders

Source PDF: all report pdf/Industrials_ AI Jitters Versus Booming Orders.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Industrials: AI Jitters Versus Booming Orders
Date:    5 hours ago
Industrials: AI Jitters Versus Booming Orders

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Industrials: AI Jitters Versus
Booming Orders
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 28 Oct 2026
View in browser
Photo by Ant Rozetsky / Unsplash

I. AI Still Boosting Capital Goods Orders
The AI investment boom has been a double-edged sword for S&P 500
Industrials this year. Back in July, we noted that the sector was the
second-best performer among the S&P 500's 11 sectors, with a 17.7% ytd
gain, as investors favored companies supplying the equipment and
infrastructure needed for the AI buildout. Since then, however, intensifying

### Page 2

AI jitters have weighed on industrial stocks, fueled by a potentially toxic
trifecta of financial constraints: elevated borrowing costs, uncertain
investment returns, and concerns about whether sufficient capital will be
available to finance everyone's ambitious AI spending plans.
The corresponding reversal has been significant. As of October 27,
Industrials was up just 8.2% ytd, compared with a 12.8% gain for the S&P
500, slipping to sixth place among the 11 sectors (chart).

The sector's stock price index is trading below its 200-day moving average
but remains on a solid multi-year uptrend (chart).
Performance within the sector has been uneven so far this year, with rail
transportation leading, while industrial machinery and aerospace and
defense lagging (chart).

### Page 3

We think the recent selloff is overdone and continue to recommend
overweighting Industrials. For starters, the sector's earnings outlook
remains upbeat. Forward earnings rose 13.6% ytd to a record high last
week (chart).
The sector's forward profit margin is also at a record high of 11.7% (chart).

### Page 4

Analysts' net revisions of their revenues and earnings estimates have also
improved significantly in recent months, with both remaining historically
elevated (chart).
Furthermore, analysts still expect double-digit earnings growth of 17.6% in
2026 and 16.5% in 2027 (chart).

### Page 5

The sector's forward P/E has fallen sharply recently, but remains above
the overall market's valuation multiple (chart).
The AI investment boom continues despite the stock market's jitters about
the AI trade. The five major hyperscalers (Amazon, Alphabet, Microsoft,
Meta, and Oracle) are projected to spend nearly $800 billion on capex this
year and more than $1 trillion in 2027. So far this week, there has been
roughly $235 billion in announced AI financing, including up to $95 billion

from Meta, a $40 billion chip-and-server leasing arrangement involving
SpaceX, and a potential $100 billion IPO by Anthropic. We therefore
continue to recommend overweighting Industrials, which should continue
to benefit from the buildout.

### Page 6

Indeed, the AI spending boom is still boosting industrial activity, with much
more in the pipeline. Consider the following:
(1) Durable goods. September durable goods orders rose 1.8% m/m, the
largest increase in five months. Orders excluding transportation increased
0.5%, while core capital goods orders (nondefense excluding aircraft), a
key gauge of business investment, rose 0.5% and 13.5% y/y to another
record high (charts).
The major components of durable goods orders are at record highs

(chart).

### Page 7

Orders for machinery necessary to operate data centers are especially
strong (chart).
Unfilled orders for computers and electronic products rose to a record
$162.4 billion in June (chart).

### Page 8

(2) Regional business surveys. All five regional Fed manufacturing
surveys are now available for October. The regional manufacturing PMI
edged down to 13.8 from September but remained comfortably in
expansion territory, suggesting that the national ISM M-PMI likely
remained above 50.0 in October (chart).
The average prices-paid index across regional Fed surveys stood at 57.0,
near levels last seen in 2022. Meanwhile, the prices-received index
climbed to 31.6, suggesting that manufacturers are increasingly passing

higher input costs on to customers (chart).

### Page 9

II. US Economy
The industrial sector isn't the only driver of the US economy showing
remarkable strength. Another key driver of economic resilience is
consumer spending. Here is more:
(1) Consumer spending. Redbook same-store sales rose 9.7% y/y
during the week of October 16. That is well above the 2025 average of
5.8% (chart).
(2) Labor market. Consumers' perceptions of labor-market conditions
improved in October. The percentage saying jobs were plentiful rose to

26.7%, the highest since April (chart).

### Page 10

The latest weekly ADP data show private-sector employment rising by an
average of 16,750 jobs per week over the four weeks through October 10,
or roughly 67,000 jobs on a monthly basis (chart). The labor market
remains in good shape.
(3) GDP. Ahead of Thursday's release of the Q3-2026 US GDP report, the
Atlanta Fed's final GDPNow estimate suggests that the economy
expanded at a robust 3.4% annualized rate during the quarter, supported
by strong consumer spending and business investment (chart).

### Page 11

clause.

## Document 358: Insurance costs hit the brakes Insurance payment growth is cooling, but the relief consumers are seeing depends heavily on who and where they are. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Insurance costs hit the brakes Insurance payment growth is cooling, but the relief consumers are seeing depends heavily on who and where they are. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13028742     1

Economy 
Insurance costs hit the brakes 
16 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Property insurance (e.g., auto, homeowners, renters) payment growth has slowed considerably, according to Bank of America 
payments data. Median payments rose just 1% year-over-year (YoY) in 2026, easing further from the 6% growth seen in 2025, 
after a roughly 12% increase in 2024, suggesting that many consumers are getting some relief from rising insurance payments. 
• However, the moderation is uneven across consumers. Bank of America payments data suggests that Gen Z continues to 
experience insurance payment growth of nearly 6%, while increases for other generations are closer to 1% to 2%. Payments 
also rose nearly 1.5% for higher-income households but edged lower for those with lower incomes. 
• Lower accident rates, easing vehicle prices, and softer major-purchase growth may be reducing cost pressures, but geography 
remains a major dividing line. Florida metros recorded declines of roughly 7% to 8%, while California and several Northeastern 
metros continued to see relatively strong increases.

Insurance payments stabilize after years of painful growth 
The burden of higher property (e.g., auto, renters, homeowners) insurance payments may be easing for some. Notably, while the 
average payment increased 3% year-over-year (YoY), the median payment was only up 1%, according to Bank of America 
payments data. This suggests that most consumers have only seen a small increase in their premiums, though there may be a 
few outliers (Exhibit 1).  
The latest data stands in contrast to just two years ago in 2024, when most consumers saw their insurance payments surge 
about 12% YoY, after a nearly 8% YoY increase in 2023.   
Exhibit 1: Insurance payments have increased only modestly through August of this year, with growth peaking in 2024 
Median and average insurance payments (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Insurance payments by age show a similar pattern, with much stronger growth in 2024 across most generations followed by a 
cooling since then (Exhibit 2). Gen Z is the outlier here, with their payments still growing at a healthy rate, increasing almost 6% 
YoY for both 2026 and 2025, after a 7% increase in 2024. Meanwhile, Millennials and Baby Boomers experienced a 2% YoY 
increase so far this year, with Gen X only seeing around a 1% rise.

Median insurance payments by age generation (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Bank of America payments data by income provides even more insight. Higher-income households paid nearly 1.5% more in 
2026 than they did a year ago, while middle-income households saw only modest increases or none at all (Exhibit 3). Conversely, 
lower-income households actually paid slightly less so far this year than they did last year.  
Exhibit 3: Higher-income households are still paying more for insurance compared to last year, while lower earners are paying less 
Median insurance payments by income cuts (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Fewer accidents and lower vehicles prices may be driving insurance payments down 
But why might insurance payments be declining at all? One thing to keep in mind is that insurance companies often raise rates 
to cover events that occurred in previous years to recover costs, while adjusting for changing risks.  
For example, the crash rate – the number of vehicle crashes per one million miles driven – suggests that there was a sharp rise 
in accidents in 2021, according to the Department of Transportation (Exhibit 4). Additionally, new and used vehicle prices 
increased significantly after the pandemic, according to Consumer Price Index (CPI) data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics 
(BLS) (Exhibit 5). So, insurers were not only facing more vehicle replacements due to a higher crash rate, but a higher cost to 
replace vehicles as well.

However, the crash rate mostly declined from 2021 to 2024, and the Department of Transportation reported that the number of 
accidents likely decreased again in 2025. Also, while the cost to maintain and repair vehicles has consistently grown since 2021, 
the price to replace vehicles peaked in 2022 and has eased since then – the latter likely a more important factor in insurance 
costs than the former. Additionally, while the CPI index for motor vehicle insurance has fallen 5% YoY, it’s possible that this may 
partially be due to rebates, according to BofA Global Research. So, the price figured into CPI may not be fully realized by the 
consumer. 
Exhibit 4: The rate of crashes is below pre- and post-pandemic

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Another factor in the divergence between a declining CPI and rising insurance payments in our data may be the growth in the 
amount of property people are insuring now. Notably though, this growth has eased in recent years. YoY growth in both new 
home and total vehicle sales peaked in mid-2023 and has grown modestly, or even declined, since (Exhibit 6). And given that 
these major purchases are still K-shaped (read more in Autos: In gear, but the “K” is still in the headlights and On the move: 
Renovation over relocation?), it could help to explain the divergence in insurance payment growth across income cohorts.  
Exhibit 6: Growth in new home and total vehicle sales peaked in mid-2023 due to a post-pandemic boom but has eased since

### Page 4

4 16 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Florida’s metros have seen the largest decline when compared to other major cities 
Examining different cities across the US also sheds light on insurance trends. Consumers in major California metros saw the 
largest increases in 2026, following near double digit growth in 2025, likely driven by widespread property damage from 
wildfires, according to BofA Global Research (Exhibit 7). Major coastal metros in the Northeast like Boston and New York also 
experienced sizable increases as well.  
Conversely, many cities throughout the Sun Belt like Houston, Nashville and Dallas saw flat or even declining insurance 
payments. BofA Global Research points to several reasons for these declines: part of the equation in this region is that the 
massive runup in the cost of property insurance and domestic migration to the region through 2024 has since eased or reversed 
course.   
Exhibit 7: California major metros have seen steady insurance payment growth, while Miami metros have seen declines 
Median insurance payments by core based statistical areas (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
In Florida, legislation that went into effect in 2023, eventually made its way into insurance prices in 2025, leading to widespread 
declines in premiums. The state also has a “lookback period” of three years, where insurance profits can be deemed too high and 
a rebate is issued to consumers. It’s possible this rebate is factoring into declines this year, as some carriers only allow rebates 
upon renewals.  
The Sunshine State was a notable standout for declines, with Orlando, Tampa and Jacksonville each down around 8% YoY in 
2026, followed closely by Miami with a 7% decrease. Additionally, Bank of America payments data suggests that Jacksonville, 
Miami and Orlando all had median insurance payments above the national level in 2023, though their payments were fell below 
that in 2026 (Exhibit 8).  
Major metros in Texas also saw easing insurance payments relative to 2023, while Seattle, San Jose, and Riverside, CA are 
among the major metros that went from below the national median to above in the three-year period ending in 2026.

## Document 413: Solvency II reforms offer limited support for bank bonds, but securitisation gains some ground

Source PDF: all report pdf/Solvency II reforms offer limited support for bank bonds, but securitisation gains some ground.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Solvency II amendments are unlikely to result in a material
increase in demand for bank bonds. Securitisations beneﬁt
most directly through lower capital charges, while covered
bonds could beneﬁt modestly from the new liquidity-buﬀer
rules
Maureen Schuller
On 30 January 2027, the amendments to the Solvency II regime
will apply, making these revisions one of the important regulatory
themes for 2027. One of the key objectives of the Solvency II

reforms is to release capital and promote long-term investments by
European insurance companies in support of the ambitions of
Europe’s Savings and Investments Union (SIU). Such capital release
could also increase the capacity of insurance companies to invest
more in corporate and bank bonds.
During the past decade, insurance companies have reduced their
bond holdings in favour of equity. Rather than an active
reallocation of assets, this partially reﬂects second-round eﬀects

related to the strong equity market growth and the rise in bond

We don’t expect the Solvency II revisions to reverse this trend,
particularly as the amendments promote long-term equity
investments. Instead, for most bank bonds, the spread-risk capital
treatment remains broadly unchanged. Unsecured and
subordinated bank bonds continue to be treated as corporate

bonds with capital charges determined by their ratings and
duration, while qualifying covered bonds keep their preferential
treatment. The impact of the Solvency II revisions therefore arises
mainly indirectly through lower overall capital requirements, while
securitisations are the only debt instruments beneﬁting directly
through a more favourable capital treatment.
Nonetheless, certain reforms are worth highlighting for their
potential impact on insurance demand for diﬀerent bank bonds.

Among these, the amendments relating to long-term equity

### Page 3

Long-term equity investments and non-
life liquidity buffers
Under Solvency II, insurance companies can classify part of their
equity holdings as long-term equity investments (LTEI), beneﬁting
from a lower 22% capital charge. To qualify, these investments
must be held for at least ﬁve years, be listed in the EEA or OECD,
meet diversiﬁcation standards and be managed under a long-term

investment policy. The insurer must also be able to avoid forced
sales for ﬁve years, even under stressed conditions.
The Solvency II amended Delegated Regulation introduces two
approaches to demonstrate that forced equity sales can be
avoided. 1) The insurer must either have suﬃcient illiquid
homogeneous life insurance liabilities with a duration of more than
9.5 years or a liquidity buﬀer exceeding 100% for non-life insurance
obligations. 2) The insurer can also apply a forced-selling test to

prove that it can generate cash inﬂows that are consistently higher
than the cash outﬂows over ﬁve years, including under stress.
The liquidity buﬀer requirements for non-life insurance activities
are similar to the Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR) requirements for
banks. The portfolio of liquid assets can comprise Level 1, Level 2A
and Level 2B assets, with the total value of Level 2A and Level 2B
assets capped at 40% and the value of the Level 2B assets at 15%.

Qualifying Level 1 assets, subject to a 0% haircut, are cash,
exposures to the ECB, multilateral development banks and
international organisations, and domestic currency exposures
to EEA central governments and central banks.
Qualifying Level 2A assets, subject to a 15% haircut, are bonds
and loans rated AA- or better that are not issued by an
insurance company or bank, and covered bonds rated AA- or
better as referred to in Article 3(1) of the European Covered

Bond Directive (CBD) (ie ‘European Covered Bonds’).
Qualifying Level 2B assets, subject to a 25% haircut, are
Simple, Transparent and Standardised (STS) securitisations (not

securitisations

### Page 5

The amended Solvency II Delegated Regulation lowers the capital
requirements for securitisation notes. This should encourage
insurers to invest more in these transactions, helping banks to
transfer risk and expand lending.
The risk factors (a and b ) for the spread risk of senior tranches
of STS securitisations will become fully aligned with the
treatment of similarly rated covered bonds as referred to in CBD
Article 3(1).
The risk factors for non-senior STS securitisations are reduced

to a similar extent, with the new capital charges for AAA-rated
non-senior notes at c.70% of the current capital charge, and for
AA-rated non-senior notes at 75% of the current requirement.
The diﬀerence in the capital treatment between STS and non-
STS securitisations is currently far more signiﬁcant for insurers
than for banks. This diﬀerence will be reduced substantially,
while the capital charge remains capped at 100%.
The current Solvency II regime makes no distinction between

senior and non-senior non-STS securitisation notes,
exaggerating the spread risk of senior compared with non-
senior tranches. Hence, lower risk factors are introduced for
senior non-STS compared to non-senior non-STS transactions.
As a result of these amendments, the diﬀerence in capital
requirements between CBD-compliant covered bonds and senior
STS securitisations will disappear from the end of January, while in
the highest rating buckets (AAA and AA) securitisations will be

treated better than unsecured bonds or third-country covered
bonds. Further down the rating scale, STS securitisations will be
treated closer to that of unsecured bonds from a capital
perspective, where corporate and bank unsecured bonds will still
beneﬁt from a more favourable capital charge than similarly rated
senior STS securitisations.
Securitisation alternatives will become
more competitive from a capital perspective

European insurance companies currently hold only about €37bn in
collateralised debt, such as asset-backed securities (ABS) and
residential mortgage-backed securities (RMBS). This is only 12% of
what they hold in covered bonds and 3% of their corporate debt
portfolio.
The Solvency II amendments may motivate insurance companies
to diversify their debt portfolios a bit further away from covered
bonds and other bank bond products towards securitisations.

However, the impact is not expected to be substantial given that
the weighted average lives of securitisation notes may not always
be well suited to the longer-term investment needs of insurance
companies.
Volatility adjustment to align spread
sensitivities more closely
Insurance companies calculate the best estimate of their liabilities
by discounting the expected future liability cash ﬂows using the
relevant risk-free interest rate term structure. To mitigate the

balance sheet impact of bond spread volatility (ie, on the asset side
of their balance sheet), insurance companies can discount their
liabilities at the risk-free rate plus a volatility adjustment (VA).
The volatility adjustment increases the
discount rate

### Page 7

Spot rate, %
Source: EIOPA, ING
For each relevant currency (or national market in the case of the
euro area), the volatility adjustment represents 65% of the spread
(S) between the interest rate on the reference portfolio and the
relevant risk-free interest rate, adjusted for a risk correction (RC). The
latter correction factor removes the part of the spread that is
attributable to expected losses or unexpected credit or other risks
to the assets. The volatility adjustment is only added to the

applicable risk-free interest rates that are not derived from
extrapolation.
For each relevant country, a country-speciﬁc adjustment may also
be added to the volatility adjustment before the application of the
65% factor if a) the risk-corrected country spread is more than twice
the risk-corrected currency spread, and b) the risk-corrected country
spread is more than 85bp. The country component in the volatility
adjustment helps mitigate excessive widening in speciﬁc countries.

Under the current regime, the representative currency and country
portfolios are constructed as uniform portfolios, even though
insurers’ actual ﬁxed-income portfolios may diﬀer substantially.
Some insurers can therefore beneﬁt excessively from the volatility
adjustment if the resulting reduction in the best estimate of their
liabilities exceeds the decline in the value of the relevant assets
when credit spreads widen.

### Page 8

For this reason, the Solvency II revisions change the calculation of
the volatility adjustment by a) making the applicable risk
corrections (RC) more spread-sensitive, and b) introducing a credit
spread sensitivity ratio (CSSR) to better align the volatility
adjustment with the insurer’s asset-liability proﬁle. The latter ratio
reﬂects the price value of a basis point of the insurer’s bond,
securitisation and loan investments versus the price value of a

basis point of the best estimate of liabilities, with a minimum of 0
and a maximum of 1.
Moreover, subject to approval by the supervisory authority, an
undertaking-speciﬁc adjustment (USA) of up to 105% may be
applied to align the risk-corrected spread on the reference portfolio
more closely with that of the insurer’s own portfolio. This must not
lead to an increase in the applied risk-adjusted spread for more
than two consecutive quarters. Moreover, when the USA is used, the

insurer cannot also increase the volatility adjustment through a
macro volatility adjustment.
The macro volatility adjustment replaces the existing country-
speciﬁc adjustment and can be added to the euro volatility
adjustment when the relevant conditions are met. It aims to
mitigate the impact of excessive spread widening in individual euro
area countries and is based on the diﬀerence between the risk-
corrected euro spread and the risk-corrected spread for the relevant

country.
Volatility adjustment calculations

The introduction of additional minimum conditions, such as
supervisory approval and the availability of adequate processes to
calculate the volatility adjustment, has also facilitated the upward

revision of the general application ratio (GAR) from 65% to 85%.
Primarily through the introduction of the credit spread sensitivity
ratio (CSSR), the volatility adjustment will better reﬂect the
relationship between each insurer’s asset and liability sensitivity to
changes in the volatility adjustment. The revision may provide
modest support for bonds that increase the asset portfolio’s spread
sensitivity, as a higher CSSR results in a larger volatility adjustment

until the ratio reaches its cap of one. The revised calculations should
also reduce the possibility for some insurers to beneﬁt
disproportionately from the volatility adjustment when their assets
are less spread-sensitive than the reference portfolio.
Conclusion
The Solvency II amendments are unlikely to trigger a material
reallocation in favour of bank bonds. Securitisations are the most
obvious beneﬁciaries of a more favourable capital treatment, while

covered bonds and unsecured bank bonds should mainly beneﬁt
indirectly from lower overall capital requirements for insurers. We

## Document 417: Swing State_ Navigating Midterm Volatility (2026-10-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Swing State_ Navigating Midterm Volatility (2026-10-23).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-23
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Swing State: Navigating 
Midterm Volatility

1 
Authors 
Laura Daniels 
Deputy Associate Director of Equity  
Research

Josh Ray 
Associate Director of Equity
